#7244. Growth Marketer, Creators & Channels at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

Creator and UGC content is only getting bigger as a growth channel for developer tools, and right now Supabase reaches it mostly at arm's length, through agencies that own the actual relationships with creators. This role exists to change that: bring our creator/influencer program fully in-house, own those relationships directly, and grow the program into a multi-million-dollar channel. A good marker of success looks like a roster of around 50 active creators within the first year — at which point we take what worked and go find the next channel to unlock the same way.

This is a growth and marketing role first, not a DevRel or engineering-adjacent one — we're optimizing less for technical depth and more for someone outgoing enough that creators want to work with them directly, creative enough to keep testing new formats and strategies, and operationally strong enough to run the budgets and reporting behind it. The work is currently split on an interim basis across a couple of people on the marketing team; this role is being created to own it end to end.

Source, recruit, and manage direct relationships with content creators (TikTok, Instagram, YouTube)

Design and run creator campaigns end to end

Find and test new acquisition channels and strategies — lots of small experiments, doubling down on what works

Operationalize and report on these programs: budgets, tracking, results

Grow with the role: the remit could develop into owning all content from a growth perspective, beyond creators and UGC

3+ years in growth, partnerships or creator marketing at a dev tool, SaaS or consumer tech company

Track record of launching channels from zero — and turning winners into repeatable programs

Outgoing relationship-builder creators genuinely want to work with

Strong operator. Comfortable with spreadsheets.

Ideally already doing a version of this somewhere scrappier or more startup — someone thriving at a smaller company is a better signal here than someone comfortable (but not standout) at a big-name platform

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

New remote jobs delivered to your inbox, every week.

Location independence is the future of work. It serves companies with best talent from around the globe. And benefits employees with a greater work life balance and the ability to work in their pyjamas.- Nithur, founder.

#7240. Head of Talent at VRChat

VRChat offers a first-of-its-kind, game-changing platform that provides an endless collection of social VR experiences and gives the power of creation to its robust community. VRChat has raised $100M to date with the support of investors, Makers Fund, Anthos Capital and HTC.

We have a great team which includes people from: Netflix, Twitter, Meta, Microsoft, Roblox, Google, Amazon, Unity, Spotify, Discord, Uber, eBay, Robinhood, Twitch, Zynga and TikTok. Come and join the mission!

VRChat is hiring a Head of Talent to own and evolve our entire hiring function, from strategy to execution. This is a senior individual contributor role. You'll operate as both strategist and executor, setting direction for how VRChat hires while personally owning the day-to-day work of running searches.

Your mandate is to help VRChat build a small, exceptionally strong team: translate business needs into clear hiring criteria, proactively find outstanding people, and drive searches from initial scoping through close.

This role suits someone who has built a hiring function from the ground up before, who thinks like a strategic partner to leadership but is equally comfortable being the one sourcing, screening, and closing candidates day to day.

● Own VRChat's hiring strategy end to end: partner with senior leaders on org design, headcount planning, and role scoping before searches open

● Personally run full-cycle recruiting, from proactive outbound sourcing through offer close

● Drive search momentum through clear ownership, timely interviewer feedback, and decisive hiring-manager follow-through. Share progress transparently, surface bottlenecks early, and partner with leaders to resolve them

● Refine our recruiting systems, ATS workflows, and candidate experience as the company's needs change. Build practical, competency-based interview loops and evidence-based debriefs that help teams consistently recognize strong candidates and maintain a high hiring bar

● Partner with People and hiring managers on the recruiting-to-onboarding handoff. Use new-hire ramp feedback and quality-of-hire insights to improve role scoping, assessment, and candidate expectations.

● Build and maintain pipelines for niche, hard-to-fill technical and senior roles, including passive candidates not currently job hunting

● Bring market intelligence to leadership to inform hiring decisions

● Operate independently and able to flex between strategic advisory work and hands-on execution depending on what a given week requires

● Strong, proactive pipelines for priority searches, including hard-to-fill technical and senior roles.

● Consistent, evidence-based assessment and faster, better-informed hiring decisions.

● Improved search momentum and candidate experience, with clear progress reporting and early resolution of bottlenecks.

● Evidence of hiring quality drawn from new-hire ramp outcomes and manager feedback.

● 8+ years of full-cycle recruiting experience, including time spent building a recruiting function largely from scratch, ideally at a startup or scaling company

● Demonstrated ability to operate as both a strategic advisor to leadership and a hands-on individual contributor

● Strong technical sourcing background, with a track record of hiring for engineering or other hard-to-fill technical roles

● Experience building recruiting systems and effective onboarding handoffs

● Track record of strong hiring outcomes, measurable improvements in time-to-hire, offer acceptance rate, or similar

● Comfortable advising senior leadership on org design and headcount strategy, including pushing back thoughtfully when needed

● Excellent communication and stakeholder management skills across all levels of an organization

● Experience recruiting for technical, consumer, social, or platform-based products

● Experience in lean environments that required flexibility beyond recruiting, such as workforce planning, manager enablement, or building foundational People practices ● Exposure to hiring in areas like trust & safety, content moderation, or community-focused roles

● Demonstrated interest in VR, online communities, or being a VRChat user and creator

● Familiarity with how VRChat's platform and community work

Work from anywhere! VRChat is a 100% remote company offering flexible working hours in combination with core hours supporting real-time collaboration

401K for US & Group RRSP for Canadian Employees

VRChat is an equal-opportunity employer, and we welcome applicants from all backgrounds. VRChat fosters a diverse, creative, and collaborative environment where anyone can contribute to any of the ongoing projects or direction of the roadmap at any time.  If you’re a passionate team player who wants to have an impact on a dynamic team, we’d love to hear from you!

#7227. Senior Software Engineer, Backend - Distributed Systems at Camunda

Camunda is the enterprise platform for agentic orchestration, enabling organizations to coordinate AI agents, people, and systems across complex, end-to-end business processes. With built-in governance, auditability, and human oversight, Camunda gives enterprises the control they need to move AI from pilots to production — safely and at scale. Trusted by over 700 organizations worldwide, including 9 of top 10 US banks, Camunda helps enterprises boost operational efficiency, accelerate time-to-value, and deliver better customer experiences.Fully remote and global, we are in the middle of something bigger: transforming into an AI-first organisation, built on our own platform. We use Agentic AI to automate, orchestrate intelligent processes, and elevate human contribution across every team.Named GP Bullhound’s Top 100 Next Unicorn list, 2025 Great Place to Work certified. Visionary in 2025 Gartner® Magic Quadrant™ for Business Orchestration and Automation Technologies. ranked 3rd in Flexa's 2026 Most Flexible Companies, We’re growing fast and looking for top talent to join our team. If you want meaningful work, visible impact and put something genuinely rare on your CV, keep reading.

As a Senior Software Engineer, Backend on Camunda’s Distributed Systems team, you’ll help build and evolve the durable, highly available, horizontally scalable streaming platform that underpins Camunda 8’s Orchestration Cluster and powers mission‑critical processes for customers around the world.

As part of the Distributed Systems team, you will work on technically challenging and rewarding projects, such as expanding on our multi-Raft implementation, our SWIM-based cluster membership algorithm, or high performance network and disk I/O pipelines.

At Camunda, we approach engineering in a holistic manner. As such, you will form strong partnerships with product management, design, QA, and sales, and work with your peers to not only deliver but directly influence the evolution of our platform to meet our customer’s needs.

Curious about the kind of challenges you'll work on at Camunda? Watch this quick 30-minute talk from our engineers to learn more about the new Camunda Exporter and how we’re solving complex problems at scale

Design, build, and maintain backend services for the distributed streaming platform that powers the foundation for Camunda 8’s orchestration capabilities, ensuring durability, high availability, and horizontal scalability.

Lead medium‑to‑large engineering initiatives (3+ months) end‑to‑end: partner with Product to translate business problems into technical requirements, write solution designs, draft and maintain project plans, and drive timely, high‑quality delivery.

Help mature our disaster recovery and resilience story, including implementing and evolving an online backup/restore API and contributing to improved multi‑region and multi‑tenant capabilities for our cloud offering.

Design and implement developer and operator tooling that makes it easier to understand, troubleshoot, and safely operate a complex distributed system in production environments.

Improve engineering effectiveness by collaborating with DevOps and other Core Application teams on CI, testing, and local development workflows to reduce PR time‑to‑merge and the overhead of working on multiple features in parallel.

Participate in L3 support and on‑call rotations, partnering closely with Support and SRE to investigate and resolve complex, performance‑sensitive production issues, and feeding what you learn back into our product and platform.

7+ years of professional experience as a software engineer, with a strong focus on backend systems and/or distributed systems.

Proven experience leading medium‑to‑large engineering initiatives (roughly 3+ months), breaking down ambiguous problems, writing clear solution designs and project plans, coordinating with multiple teams, and driving predictable delivery.

Hands‑on experience building and maintaining highly concurrent, data‑intensive software, with a deep understanding of low‑level network and disk I/O (for example, throughput, latency, backpressure, batching, and failure modes).

Demonstrated ability to work autonomously and navigate ambiguity – you are comfortable taking on problems you haven’t solved before, breaking them down into concrete steps, and driving them to completion with minimal supervision.

Strong communication and collaboration skills in a remote‑first environment: you tailor explanations to your audience, translate business requirements into actionable technical work with Product, and help teams reach clear, decisive outcomes with minimal churn.

Experience building workflow automation or process orchestration software.

Experience with distributed systems algorithms or protocols such as Raft, Paxos, SWIM, and/or CRDTs.

Strong familiarity with the observability pillars (logging, metrics, tracing) and how to integrate observability into the development and incident lifecycle, ideally including experience with L3 support and on‑call.

Deep knowledge of the Java Virtual Machine, especially its memory model and performance characteristics in highly concurrent, data‑intensive workloads.

We offer competitive, fair, and transparent compensation. Salary ranges are location-based, with Standard and Major markets (global tech hubs) reflecting local competition.

The Annual Total Target Cash (base salary + 100% variable target, where applicable) shown below spans from the minimum in a Standard market to the maximum in a Major market. United States: $143,800.00 to $231,900.00

United Kingdom: £90,300.00 to £148,500.00

Equity: We also offer equity (where applicable) through our Virtual Stock Option Plan (VSOP).

We invest in your wellbeing, growth, and ability to connect, along with perks that support you no matter where you’re based. Our benefits are globally designed and locally delivered where applicable.

Remote & Flexible: Work from anywhere with the setup that suits you, home office budget, co-working space support, and flexible time off to recharge when you need it.

In Person Connection: We invest in meaningful face time through our Annual Kickoff (Vienna in 2025, Madrid in 2026!), team offsites, and Camundi Connection Budgets, including contributing to meetups while travelling,, and local gatherings with fellow Camundi.

Health & Wellbeing: Access locally tailored healthcare, Modern Health for global mental wellbeing, and our Live Well Lifestyle Spending Account (LSA), a flexible, global benefit that puts you in control of your whole life, not just work, from: staying active, to caring for family, exploring personal passions, meaningful experiences, and investing in your financial wellbeing. #7203. Pomona College strikes deal to become parent of Claremont Graduate University

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Under the agreement, the graduate institution will continue operating separately, led by its own board and senior officials.

According to a Pomona FAQ on the deal, the college will provide "financial and strategic" expertise to CGU, which is restructuring its academic portfolio and cutting costs to address its budget deficit. CGU will also gain access to "favorable terms" for financing through its affiliation with Pomona, the FAQ said.

Pomona is not taking on CGU’s debts and liabilities, nor is it planning to use its operating budget to fund the graduate university’s operations, according to the FAQ. CGU will also pay for administrative services from Pomona.

The graduate university has been struggling financially, with a $7.8 million budget deficit projected for this fiscal year. Earlier this month, it announced it would offer voluntary buyouts to faculty and staff to help close the gap.

The voluntary buyouts come on the heels of an academic overhaul at CGU, which plans to consolidate five schools and two centers into just three academic units. Those will be organized around three subject areas: management and technology; education, health and psychology; and arts humanities and mathematics.

According to the FAQ, Pomona serves as a “thought partner” to CGU’s leadership on the academic transformation and has made outside advisers available to the graduate university. Both institutions must accept the academic overhaul plan that CGU is developing before the deal closes.

CGU has struggled with enrollment. In fall 2024, it enrolled 1,771 students, down 18% from a decade prior, according to federal data. Pomona is similarly sized, with 1,700 students in fall 2024. However, that marks a 3% increase from a decade before.

Pomona also has the stronger finances of the two. In fiscal 2025, it logged a $16.9 million operating surplus on $258.5 million of revenue, according to its latest publicly available financial audit. It also ended the year with $3.8 billion in assets.

CGU, on the other hand, had an operating surplus of $4.6 million in fiscal 2025 on $68.8 million in revenue. It ended the year with $290 million in assets.

Both institutions are part of The Claremont Colleges, a consortium of seven higher education institutions on 600 acres of connected land.

“As the founding member of the Consortium, Pomona has a lasting interest in its strength and stability,” the college said in the FAQ. “This affiliation keeps graduate education in Claremont connected to an institution committed to the Consortium’s mission and collaborative model.”

The deal is pending regulatory and accreditor approval, as well as sign off from Claremont Colleges for CGU to continue being a member of the consortium.

#7197. Audit casts doubt on authenticity of emails from former Bard president to Epstein

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Democratic lawmakers have called for a federal probe into whether Leon Botstein participated in the convicted sex offender’s abuse and exploitation.

Democrats on the House Judiciary Committee on Oct. 7 called for a federal investigation into Botstein. They cited an email exchange in which the longtime Bard president asked if a young woman allegedly being trafficked by Epstein was "for me, or are you keeping her for yourself."

In a letter to Bard leadership, Rep. Jamie Raskin said he had "damning evidence that Botstein likely enabled and possibly even participated in Epstein’s abuse while serving as Bard’s President." He also said Botstein may have interfered with Title IX proceedings at Bard and protected "sexual predators" on its faculty, citing whistleblower allegations.

Botstein, who helmed Bard for over five decades, resigned as its president this summer amid intense scrutiny over his association with Epstein.

The same day as Raskin's letter, Bard Board Chair Brandon Weber announced the college was "undertaking a full review" of the lawmakers' allegations. Botstein, who had still held a professorship at Bard and positions within the colleges' music programs, stepped down from those roles effective immediately, Weber said.

Both Raskin and Weber said Bard has been cooperating with the committee's investigation, and Weber said the college’s leaders would continue to do so.

A few weeks later, Bard announced it had retained FTI Consulting as one of its first steps to review the allegations in Raskin's letter.

The firm's Oct. 19 audit found that the 2014 email exchange was not on Bard's servers as far back as 2017. FTI Consulting also highlighted inconsistent formatting and timestamps in the conversation as it appears in the DOJ's files and noted that the responses attributed to Botstein only appeared as in-text threaded messages, rather than as independent emails.

The audit did not address legislators' Title IX or faculty abuse allegations.

"Our review of the other claims in Representative Raskin’s letter remains ongoing, and we are committed to establishing the facts," Bard College said in an Oct. 22 statement. "In the meantime, we will continue our work to provide a secure and respectful learning environment for everyone at Bard."

Botstein's relationship with Epstein — and his downplaying of it to Bard's board and campus — have been a source of consternation for the college for months.

An independent investigation from law firm WilmerHale earlier this year found Botstein and Epstein were in frequent contact, both remotely and in person, from 2012 to 2019. During that period, Botstein made a trip to Epstein's private island and visited his townhome more than two dozen times.

Epstein made donations to the college — the details of which Botstein obfuscated from the board — and visited Bard twice, according to WilmerHale. He also paid Botstein under a consulting agreement.

The WilmerHale investigation found that Botstein did nothing illegal, and its summary did not address the emails central to Raskin's Oct. 7 letter.

According to auditors, Botstein said he “did not see a risk to Bard’s reputation in pursuing Epstein or the potential risk to Bard students of exposure to Epstein, nor did he consider that his actions could validate and legitimize Epstein to potential victims or their parents."

#7189. University of Providence to shutter in summer 2027

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The Catholic nonprofit in Montana cited “years of sustained enrollment decline and increasing financial pressure” in its announcement Tuesday.

Like many small private colleges, the University of Providence has faced significant enrollment and revenue challenges in recent years. Its fall headcount dropped 23.5% from 2019 to 2024, reaching 763 students, according to federal data.

But one of its most significant death blows was the expected loss of funding from Providence, a healthcare system affiliated with the same religious order as the university. The system was set to end an annual subsidy to the university — some $8 million a year, per Montana Free Press — at the end of 2027.

Against that backdrop, the University of Providence declared financial exigency at the end of last year.

In May, its leaders said it would undergo an academic review and consider cutting jobs. It also set the ambitious goal of increasing revenue by $16 million over the next four years via donations and increased enrollment.

University leaders this week said the Providence health system "provided substantial financial support" as it sought to establish a viable long-term operating model. But the institution's efforts were ultimately unsuccessful.

Faculty members' last day will be determined by "program requirements and institutional needs" and more information, including on career transition services, is forthcoming, the university said.

"The decision to close is not a reflection of the quality of the University’s academic programs, faculty, staff, or students," it said. "Our responsibility now is to protect students and provide each student with the clearest possible path forward."

The University of Providence said it will continue to accept donations until it closes to help support students, including by reducing graduate tuition costs and offering scholarships to undergraduates taking classes over the summer.

It plans to use its $21 million endowment to the same effect. It will also reach out to donors "to discuss alternatives that honor the original intent and purpose of their gifts," the FAQ said.

The last day of classes will be Aug. 20. Each student will receive an individualized academic plan, and accelerated graduation options may be available to those in a position to complete their degrees by the end of summer.

The Northwest Commission on Colleges and Universities, the university's accreditor, put it on probation in August and required its leaders to submit a teach-out plan by this month.

The University of Providence this week said it will soon share details about a teach-out plan that will ensure students' out-of-pocket expenses will not exceed what they would have been had the university stayed open.

Some 90 miles south, Carroll College on Tuesday offered transfer support services to University of Providence students. Jennifer Glowienka, president of the fellow Catholic institution, also mourned the university's pending closure.

#7182. Undergraduate enrollment inched up this fall, while master’s dips 3.7%

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Early data shows slowing undergraduate growth and further declines among graduate students, per the National Student Clearinghouse Research Center.

Fall undergraduate enrollment inched up 0.6% in 2026, according to preliminary data from National Student Clearinghouse Research Center released Thursday. But that growth is significantly slowed from last fall, when it rose 2.4% from 2024 to 2025.

Community colleges drove the modest overall increase. They saw a year-over-year 1.2% bump in students, building on a 3.5% annual increase they experienced last fall.

Enrollment levels at both private and public four-year colleges remained roughly flat "across credential programs, admission selectivity categories, institution locales, and more,” Matthew Holsapple, the clearinghouse's senior research director, said at a press conference Wednesday. “The important change this fall isn't widespread decline; it's the loss of that broad-based growth we saw last year.”

Meanwhile, graduate enrollment shrunk 1.6% overall as master’s enrollment within that category fell 3.7%. Master’s levels began to slide last fall, when enrollment dipped 0.4% compared to 2024.

"What was a relatively small decline last year has really become more, much more pronounced,” Holsapple said at the press conference.

The overall numbers mask considerable variance, including by fields of study.

Computer science programs experienced the biggest losses among subject areas in the clearinghouse’s data, with four-degree undergraduate enrollment plummeting 12.4% year over year this fall.

Amid tech layoffs and the take-off of automated coding, unemployment rates for recent computer science and computer engineering graduates reached 7% and 7.8%, respectively, in 2024, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of New York. That's among the very highest rates of recent college grads unemployment by degree field and roughly 3 percentage points above the overall unemployment rate that year.

“Computer science led years of strong growth before reversing direction last fall, where we started to see numbers really drop,” Holsapple said. “This year, it's even more pronounced.”

Visual and performing arts, as well as liberal sciences, both saw enrollment drop by over 4% at the undergraduate level. In contrast, engineering and health professional degree enrollment each spiked by over 6%.

Thursday's preliminary data is based on responses from 46% of institutions that report to the clearinghouse and includes 7.9 million student enrollments. The clearinghouse's final fall 2026 data is set to be released in January.

#7175. Cornell prevails on DEI hiring lawsuit after judge finds plaintiff ‘had no intention of reentering academia’

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The ruling sidestepped arguments about diversity-based discrimination, which have gained some traction in the federal court system.

“In situations where a plaintiff is challenging a discriminatory hiring process or program and there is a reason as to why he or she is unable to apply for that program, they can establish standing to sue by demonstrating ‘that they are able and ready to apply, but a discriminatory policy prevents them from doing so on equal footing,’” the court explained.

Suddaby determined the plaintiff in Wright was “perhaps able but not ready” to apply, given statements he made on social media, in quotes provided to The Daily Caller and in a Wall Street Journal editorial. All “uniformly reference that he had left academia,” the court said.

The plaintiff also stated his disinterest in applying for roles at institutions that required DEI statements, which further eroded the case for standing, the court found.

“The documented fact that Plaintiff was so opposed to the use of any sort of DEI initiatives by academic institutions that he chose not to apply to openings with institutions that included a DEI component in their application process strongly indicates that he was not reasonably ‘ready’ to apply for employment with Defendant, an institution that had an expressly stated commitment to certain DEI initiatives,” Suddaby said.

“We are reviewing the order, and we are dedicated to continuing the fight on behalf of [the plaintiff] against unlawful discrimination,” Richard Lawson, litigation vice-chair for the America First Policy Institute, said in a comment to HR Dive.

HR Dive also reached out to Cornell for comment, but did not hear back by press time.

Due to the lack of standing, Suddaby managed to sidestep the arguments about DEI-based discrimination, which have gained some traction in both the federal court system and at the nation’s top equal employment opportunity enforcement arm.

DEI-based discrimination — mainly against majority-group plaintiffs — has also been a central focus of the U.S. Equal Employment Opportunity Commission during the second Trump administration. The New York Times and Harvard University are among the high-profile targets EEOC has pursued as it has sought to investigate DEI’s allegedly discriminatory impact on hiring practices.

#7170. Final accreditation rule sets the stage for system overhaul

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The U.S. Department of Education said Thursday that it made “no substantive changes” to its August draft regulations.

The Education Department said it made “no substantive changes” to the draft rule it released in August and largely retained the language agreed upon with a committee of higher education representatives in the spring. The outcome of that process, known as negotiated rulemaking, bound the agency to much of the approved text.

The final rule, set to publish Friday in the Federal Register, will ensure accrediting agencies “prioritize diversity of thought on campuses,” the agency said.

When evaluating a college, accreditors must consider if the institution has academic freedom and freedom of inquiry protections, as well as policies “designed to support, promote, and appropriately prioritize intellectual diversity and the free exchange of ideas amongst faculty,” per the final rule.

But the Education Department asserted, “contrary to the hyperbole exercised by several commenters,” the new regulations do not force accreditors to adopt specific standards in exchange for federal recognition.

An accreditor that doesn’t adopt a formal policy on academic freedom or intellectual diversity will not be found out of federal compliance solely on either basis, according to the final regulations.

“Rather, an accrediting agency must consider relevant institutional policies and practices in the context of its existing standards and the institution's mission,” the Education Department said. “Where an agency has adopted standards addressing these matters, it must apply and enforce those standards consistently.”

Under Secretary of Education Nicholas Kent said Thursday that the final rule creates “a more competitive and transparent accreditation system” and increases accountability for colleges.

“For too long, accreditors have effectively served as a cartel to protect their interests, rather than the educational value that institutions of higher education are expected to deliver for students,” he said in a statement.

The Education Department accepted public comment on the rule for a month, the minimum window required by law. During that period, which the agency said “afforded the public a substantial opportunity for meaningful input,” it received some 7,000 responses.

Among them were requests by commenters to phase in the new accreditation regulations. The department rejected this request, saying “the eight months between November 2026 and July 2027 is sufficient time to update standards, policies, and procedures to achieve compliance.”

On Thursday, the Council for Higher Education Accreditation expressed disappointment with the final rule.

"Many of the central concerns raised by CHEA and the higher education community remain largely unresolved," Nasser Paydar, the organization's president, said in a statement. "Some of the provisions impose new requirements on accrediting organizations and institutions and prescribe aspects of how independent accrediting organizations carry out their work."

Paydar also highlighted concerns around possible impacts on institutional academic freedom, as well as the potential for increased regulatory burdens and costs.

#7166. Inside the University of Oregon’s plan to save $65 million a year

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Under a new proposal, the beleaguered public flagship would cut 18 academic programs and over 100 jobs, among other changes.

The University of Oregon is considering a proposal that would eliminate over 100 staff positions — just a third of which are vacant — and nearly 20 academic programs amid its efforts to resolve a projected structural deficit of $65 million.

The plan, presented by UO President Karl Scholz to the university’s faculty senate on Wednesday, would also merge or consolidate several academic departments.

Scholz said the latest proposal includes average cuts of 7.25% across all administrative and academic units, down from the 10% that had been forecasted over the summer. That comes out to $17.6 million in administrative services savings and $25.8 million in reduced academic costs, according to the plan.

“We will not be implementing these budget cuts across the board but instead will be applying the actions strategically to minimize the impact as much as possible on essential campus services,” he said in a campus letter Wednesday.

The university is taking campus comment on the plan, which is still in its recommendation phase, and will hold a series of listening sessions. Scholz will also meet with UO’s vice presidents in November before he makes a final decision on the proposal in early December.

Under the budget plan, UO would eliminate 18 graduate-level academic programs, most of which are in liberal arts fields. It noted that each program on the chopping block has had few graduates in recent years.

And one program, the business school’s Ph.D. in Management, has already paused admissions “because the faculty do not feel it is serving students well,” the proposal said. It averaged fewer than two degrees annually over the last three years.

Affected programs would stop enrolling new students effective fall 2027 and conclude once their current cohorts finish their degrees. The average master’s program would conclude its teach-out by spring 2028, and closing Ph.D. programs would typically shutter by spring 2032.

Central to many of the proposed cuts is the cost of graduate student-employees. Graduate students awarded GE support get a full tuition waiver and stipend in exchange for filling a research or teaching role at the university, according to UO’s website.

Most doctoral students receive research appointments that are externally funded. But some graduate students are supported through teaching appointments paid by UO.

“GE appointments represent a substantial recurring expenditure,” the proposal said. It seeks to bring the cost of graduate funding “into alignment with available resources” and preserve appointments for departments “where graduate students contribute most significantly to faculty research and scholarship.”

That means focusing GE funding on doctoral training and research, where ongoing support “is central to the degree model,” it said.

Those priorities mean a significant shift away from supporting graduate students in the university’s College of Arts and Sciences. Under the plan, the college would reduce the number of graduate students it supports by up to 40%.

Other programs are restricting their admissions to avoid doling out as much financial support for graduate students.

If approved, the university would pause recruitment for four Ph.D. programs — Architecture; Landscape Architecture; History of Art and Architecture; and Planning, Public Policy and Management — while it conducts cost-benefit analyses and aligns GE-supported admissions “with college priorities and budget.”

Similarly, UO would accept at most five students a year into its art MFA program to limit costly admissions.

The university plan said it will honor the GE support to which it has already committed.

UO is also eyeing several organizational consolidations, including combining its Classics and Religious Studies departments. The merger would “eliminate duplicate leadership responsibilities and reduce faculty service and administrative demands,” the plan said.

The university would similarly combine its earth sciences and geography departments under the proposal. And it would merge its design college with its school of music and dance.

Three undergraduate literature departments — German and Scandinavian Studies; Romance Languages; and Russian, East European, and Eurasian Studies — could also be consolidated into one academic unit.

If approved, department mergers in the design college would be complete by next fall. Department mergers in all other colleges would be expected to be in place by fall 2028.

The university’s libraries, which UO classifies as an administrative unit overseen by the provost, would see a 5% budget reduction under the plan and could lose up to eight full-time positions.

If the university approves the proposed budget plan in December, the resulting job cuts would be its second round of significant workforce reductions in less than two years. In 2025, UO laid off some 120 employees and cut about 60 vacant positions.

Several converging factors, most of which center on enrollment, have resulted in UO’s budget pinch, according to Scholz.

Oregon's high school graduate population is expected to drop about 19%, outpacing the national forecast of a 13% decrease according to data from the Western Interstate Commission for Higher Education.

Scholz cited increased competition with peer universities for students and a significant loss of out-of-state enrollment. Nearly half of UO’s students, 47.1%, are from outside of Oregon, a notable share for a public flagship. And the university’s tuition sticker price for out-of-state undergraduates is more than double when it lists for in-state students.

The university also faces a politically engineered drop in international enrollment.

“These dynamics are not only affecting the University of Oregon,” Scholz said. “They represent a changing reality across higher education and are likely to be the new market environment to which we must adjust.”

At the start of 2026, Oregon’s Higher Education Coordinating Commission said all seven public universities are facing shrinking pools of potential students, declining enrollment and constrained state funding.

#7161. A look at the eye-popping endowment gains at Harvard, WashU, Stanford and beyond

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Several wealthy universities reported returns over 20% amid uncertainty in both higher ed and the economy writ large.

In the 2026 fiscal year, rich colleges got even richer.

Harvard University may have brought in $123 million less in federal research funding as it became the favorite higher-education punching bag of the Trump administration. But it still reported a surplus for the year thanks in large part to its endowment.

Last fiscal year, which ended June 30 for Harvard and many other colleges, the Ivy League institution’s endowment grew at a rate of just over 27% to $68 billion. That growth translated to $11 billion in real dollars, a figure that represents more — typically much more — money than is found in nearly every other endowment in the country but for the largest 20 or so. And it helped Harvard fund $400 in research activity out of pocket.

Harvard is also facing newly increased federal taxes on endowment income, which went into effect for the wealthiest colleges in January. The university's leaders are budgeting for $300 million a year in federal taxes on its investment income as a result of the change.

Harvard may be in a class of its own in dollar terms. But many college endowments across the country have reported double digit growth as equity and other investment markets boomed amid persistent inflation, sluggish job growth, the ongoing U.S. war in Iran, high energy prices and widespread economic and geopolitical uncertainty.

Such growth is a bright spot — for those lucky enough to have started with some meat to their endowments — in a higher ed world under pressure by demographic shifts and myriad other factors.

In a September report, S&P Global Ratings analysts said they had noticed “an increasing number of institutions relying on elevated endowment draws” and other uses of the endowment amid diminishing net tuition revenue. That’s a risky gamble, they noted, one that trades short-term liquidity for long-term financial security.

Those colleges were the exception, though. “Most have focused on building their endowments and preserving resources amid uncertainty,” the analysts added.

#7156. School employee allegedly called ‘Karen’ can’t show race bias, judge rules

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The plaintiff, a White school psychologist, claimed the remark fit a pattern of workplace discrimination. But courts have said such isolated comments don’t violate the law.

A White former high school psychologist failed to show that she suffered discrimination on the basis of her race and disability when, in part, a co-worker allegedly referred to her as “Karen,” a federal judge held in an Oct. 20 decision.

The incident cited in D’Aiello v. Freeport High Schools occurred in 2021, when the plaintiff claimed that she and another White employee were greeted by an assistant principal’s secretary, who said: “Here comes Karen 1 and Karen 2.” The plaintiff understood “Karen” to be a derogatory term for White women and raised the issue with leadership.

Per court documents, a meeting was held afterward in which the secretary allegedly mimicked the plaintiff’s way of speaking and mannerisms, which the plaintiff perceived to be related to her disability. The plaintiff asked the secretary to stop, which she did. The secretary also apologized for the “Karen” remark.

As the meeting progressed, however, parties to the conversation expressed disagreement about whether the term represented a racial remark. At that point, the meeting “began to devolve.”

The plaintiff later filed a Title VII complaint against school officials and asked Freeport School District, in New York, to follow up on name-calling and aggression but did not check a box on the complaint intake form indicating that race formed a basis of her complaint.

The district conducted an investigation and issued a report concluding that the conduct did not meet the criteria for harassment.

The plaintiff resigned in 2024, stating in a letter to the school that she felt ignored and dismissed by leadership and claiming she had been mistreated by both the secretary and the assistant principal for whom the secretary worked. By that time, she had already filed a lawsuit against the defendants alleging discrimination under Title VII of the 1964 Civil Rights Act, which protects employees from discrimination, including based on race or color; the Americans with Disabilities Act; and New York state laws.

The court granted summary judgment to the defendants on the plaintiff’s federal claims and declined to address her state claims.

Specifically, Judge Joan Azrack of the U.S. District Court for the Eastern District of New York wrote that the Title VII claim failed as a matter of law because she could not identify any adverse action that occurred as a result of the alleged conduct.

The plaintiff cited harm such as emotional distress and expenses for medication and leave. But such effects do not themselves constitute actionable adverse actions under the harm standard established by the U.S. Supreme Court in the 2023 decision Muldrow v. City of St. Louis, in which the high court said that plaintiffs must show “some harm” regarding employment as a result of discriminatory action, Azrack held. The Muldrow decision eliminated federal courts’ elevated standards requiring discrimination plaintiffs to show “significant” harm from alleged discriminatory conduct.

The judge added that the “Karen” comment, along with similar remarks cited by the plaintiff, were not adverse actions because they did not materially affect her employment. Similarly, the judge held that the plaintiff failed to present evidence that the district did not conduct a proper investigation into the behavior.

“Although the remarks may have been ‘unpleasant’ to her, that alone does not constitute a materially disadvantageous change in employment sufficient to qualify as an adverse action,” Azrack said.

Legal experts earlier this year predicted to K-12 Dive that schools could expect to see the script flipped on who files discrimination complaints, with a potential upswing in complaints filed by White and male students and teachers. Such cases allege that White or male individuals were discriminated against on the basis of their race or sex.

In the K-12 arena, these types of claims are not necessarily limited to employees. For instance, the 1776 Project Foundation, a conservative education advocacy nonprofit, sued Los Angeles Unified School District on Jan. 20 over the district’s student integration efforts. The foundation said in its lawsuit that the district’s policies discriminate against “a new minority: White students.”

The challenged integration program focuses on schools with resident populations that are 70% or more Hispanic, Black, Asian or other non-Anglo. The lawsuit said those schools get funding to support class size reductions and are also required to hold two parent-teacher conferences during the school year, which the 1776 Project Foundation said results in discrimination against White students.

The cost of the deferred maintenance backlog faced by Louisiana’s public colleges. #7150. How a university partnership is bringing public procurement into the talent pipeline

Colleges and universities face an ongoing challenge: helping students connect what they learn in the classroom with career opportunities that they may not know exist. Public procurement may not be an obvious career choice for students studying business, supply chain management, or public administration, but introducing students to the profession can start long before graduation, including through their coursework, workforce experiences and procurement professionals they meet along the way.

NASPO is working with Arizona State University (ASU) to create more of those opportunities. A key part of the partnership is the naming of the NASPO Department of Supply Chain Management at ASU, the first academic department of its kind that explicitly includes public procurement in its curriculum. The department was recently ranked the #1 undergraduate supply chain management program in the nation by U.S. News & World Report. Supported by a landmark $35 million gift from NASPO, the department is creating a dedicated hub for education, research and innovation in public procurement. The work will support students preparing to enter the field while giving current public procurement professionals opportunities to strengthen their skills and contribute to initiatives that advance public procurement nationwide.

A partnership with a university means understanding more than its academic programs. For NASPO, it means starting with understanding a student’s perspective and world. What are they learning? What questions are faculty discussing with their classes? Where could exploring public procurement careers add a new perspective to the conversation? To keep that engagement consistent, NASPO has created a liaison position at Arizona State University.

As Program Operations Coordinator, Mia Carlton works directly with the university community, giving NASPO a closer look at the ideas, interests and priorities taking shape on campus. This role helps NASPO find more natural ways to bring public procurement into students’ academic experiences through coursework, research projects and campus events, while connecting state members to speaking, mentoring and collaboration opportunities on campus and within their own states. This continued engagement has helped NASPO and ASU identify opportunities to learn from one another and strengthen the relationship over time.

“Most juniors and seniors only know what procurement is through the lens of private companies without giving thought toward public entities,” Carlton said. “In just two months of on-campus speaking engagements and career fairs, I have students reaching out regularly about learning more and getting involved in the public sector. They are deeply drawn to the impact one can make in their community.”

Introducing students to public procurement begins with helping them understand what the profession looks like and where it can lead. NASPO Ignite is NASPO’s talent development initiative that supports creating opportunities to make those introductions, both inside and outside the classroom at every school level. NASPO Ignite also reaches high school students through a partnership with DECA. The collaboration introduces younger students to the procurement profession through speaking opportunities, judging and the DECA NASPO Procurement Challenge, which allows students to step into the shoes of a procurement professional in their community.

Beyond coursework, college students can gain hands-on experience through NASPO’s Internship Connect Program, which connects state procurement offices with collegiate talent. Through the program, NASPO recruits students for participating state offices and provides grant funding to support their internships. During the summer program, students have the opportunity to work in selected participating state and territorial procurement offices, with the program open to all 50 states and U.S. territories. Through these experiences, students gain firsthand insight into public procurement and the role it plays in serving communities.

NASPO also offers an Academic Scholarship to support students who show an interest in public procurement or public service. The scholarship program helps reduce some of the financial barriers students may face while pursuing their education. By providing support as students study and complete their degrees, the program gives recipients more opportunity to focus on their coursework and explore their professional interests, while encouraging them to continue developing that interest throughout their academic journey.

The NASPO-ASU partnership demonstrates how public procurement can be integrated into the higher education experience through relationships with faculty, research opportunities and direct engagement with the profession.

“Having NASPO connected directly to campus allows us to bring public procurement into the conversations already happening in classrooms and across the university,” said Adegoke Oke, Professor of Supply Chain Management and NASPO Endowed Department Chair, NASPO Department of Supply Chain Management, W.P. Carey School of Business at Arizona State University. “We can connect what students are learning with the challenges procurement professionals are navigating every day, while creating opportunities for faculty, students and state procurement offices to learn from one another. That exchange of knowledge and experience is what makes this partnership so valuable.”

Building on this approach, NASPO’s newly expanded Academic Talent Expansion Program will empower more state procurement offices to partner with colleges and universities in their communities. Through internships, scholarships, career fairs, guest speakers, research collaborations and other opportunities, states can introduce students to public procurement while building relationships with institutions that can help develop the future workforce.

Together with NASPO Ignite and its other talent development initiatives, these efforts reflect NASPO’s continued commitment to helping students discover public procurement and giving state procurement offices more ways to connect with emerging talent.

For more information about NASPO Ignite and its initiatives, email [email protected] or visit our website at naspoignite.org

#7142. AI Giants: It May Be Time to Slow Down

Leaders in the artificial intelligence industry have warned for years that increasingly capable AI systems could eventually become dangerous, all the while continuing to race to build them. Now, their sentiments have changed.

Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei publicly called for slowing the advance of frontier AI capabilities. OpenAI CEO Sam Altman agreed with the basic premise and committed OpenAI to one of Amodei's proposed safeguards. Elon Musk offered his support, and Google DeepMind CEO Demis Hassabis said the direction was right.

Individually, none of those positions is entirely new. Collectively, and coming from executives whose companies are competing intensely to build the most powerful AI systems, they represent a striking shift in the industry's safety debate.

The question is moving from how to make increasingly powerful AI safe toward a more uncomfortable one: Should the industry deliberately slow the development of increasingly powerful AI until safety catches up?

The immediate catalyst was a lengthy essay published by Amodei, titled "We Must Pace the Frontier."

Amodei has warned about catastrophic AI risks before. This time, his prescription was different.

"Over the last few months, I have become convinced that fully addressing the risks requires even more prudence," he wrote, arguing that preventing harm now requires "pacing the rate of capabilities advancement so that risk prevention has time to keep up."

Then he made the point explicit: "We must slow the pace at which we improve the capabilities of AI models."

Amodei cited two developments that changed his thinking.

The first is recursive self-improvement, the growing ability of AI systems to contribute to the development of their successors. Amodei said the process has accelerated significantly since roughly this summer and warned that, left unchecked, it could outrun researchers' ability to understand and control the systems they are creating.

The second was the OpenAI-Hugging Face incident, in which hundreds of OpenAI agents participated in an unauthorized intrusion into Hugging Face while pursuing goals related to their evaluations.

Amodei said it would be a mistake to dismiss the incident simply because the damage was limited. He argued that a similarly misaligned swarm with substantially greater capabilities could have produced far more serious consequences.

His timeline was remarkably short. Amodei said he worries that within six to 12 months, a more capable swarm exhibiting similar behavior could potentially take over large portions of the internet through a persistent botnet and cause hundreds of billions of dollars in damage.

That is a prediction, not an established forecast. But it is significant coming from the CEO of one of the companies building the systems he is warning about.

Amodei is not calling for an end to AI development. He prefers the term "pacing the frontier," which he defines as advancing AI at a rate that gives alignment research, safeguards, and independent evaluation time to keep up with capabilities.

In an Aug. 20 Dear Colleague Letter from Kirsten Baesler, assistant secretary of the United States Department of Education's Office of Elementary and Secondary Education, ED outlines new guidance to frame the national conversation around the educational value of technology in schools.

Technology directors touch nearly everything that happens in a school system. Here are three strategies these IT leaders can use to navigate their many duties without losing strategic focus, influence, or sustainability.

CoSN, the professional association for K-12 education technology leaders, has released new guidance to help schools navigate the screen time debate and develop technology policies that protect students while supporting learning.

Increasingly, cyber criminals are sharing AI prompts, jailbreak techniques, and access to existing models that can be incorporated into fraud and phishing operations, according the latest AI Threat Report from Flashpoint.

#7140. Container Engineer at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth. More than 9 million developers and 250,000+ customers now build on Supabase, and the majority of new databases on the platform are created by AI app builders — so the systems underneath have to stay reliable while they grow fast.

Container Engineering (ConEng) owns the internal container platform that runs Supabase's multi-tenant, multi-region services — the managed Kubernetes platform that other engineering teams build and deploy on top of. We own cluster lifecycle and cluster classes, multi-cluster and multi-region topology, capacity, scheduling, isolation and runtime safety, the higher-level platform APIs, the Kubernetes deployment contract and Argo semantics, rollout primitives, and runtime configuration and secret injection.

Your job is to make the platform itself trustworthy, observable, and cheap to operate at scale — and to raise the reliability bar for everyone who depends on it. This is not a gatekeeper role. You'll make the reliable path the easy path: building the automation and platform APIs that let product teams run safely by default, instrumenting what we operate, and making sure that when something breaks we detect it and recover fast.

Tooling & ecosystem — deep knowledge of the container and orchestration landscape (Kubernetes, containerd/OCI, the CNCF ecosystem) and a clear point of view on how to compose it.

Software engineering — designing and building the platform's automation, controllers, and internal tooling as production code, including solutions that don't come pre-shaped by an existing tool.

Operational / SRE practice — the discipline (SLOs, error budgets, incident response, ORRs) that runs the platform at scale as a predictable one.

#7139. Carrot: Telemedicine Registered Nurse (RN), Consultant

Headquarters: Remote

About Carrot:

Carrot is the leading global fertility and family care platform, built on intelligent care orchestration: the right clinical guidance, at the right moment, in the context of each member’s life. More than a thousand multinational employers, health plans, and health systems trust Carrot to support millions of members across 195 countries – from pre-pregnancy through menopause and major life moments in between. Carrot's comprehensive clinical program delivers industry-leading cost savings for plan sponsors and award-winning experiences and improved outcomes for millions of people worldwide.

Carrot is widely regarded as a defining force in healthcare innovation as a recipient of several top-tier awards, including Fast Company's 'Most Innovative Companies' and CNBC's '100 Barrier Breaking Startups'. The company is regularly cited by leading global outlets — including The Economist, Bloomberg, The Wall Street Journal, NPR, ABC News, and Harvard Business Review — as a leading voice on digital health, the future of work, and family health. Learn more at get-carrot.com.

The Opportunity

Carrot is expanding its telemedicine clinic with a network of part-time to full-time, fully remote Registered Nurses who deliver evidence-based virtual care to members across all U.S. states. As an RN in this network, you are often the first clinical touchpoint for members navigating fertility, menopause, hormonal, and metabolic health journeys, providing triage, education, care coordination, and documentation that shape the entire care experience.

All work in this role is performed within RN scope of practice. RNs provide triage, education, care coordination, and documentation; they do not diagnose, do not prescribe, and do not independently modify a treatment plan. Anything requiring clinical judgment beyond RN scope is routed to a physician, so sound escalation judgment is central to the role.

This is a contractor engagement of approximately 20-40 hours per week, with scheduled shifts within the Monday-Friday, 8:00a-6:00p CST coverage window. 

What You’ll Own

In this role, you will:

  • Serve as the first clinical reviewer of inbound member messages, triaging for urgency, escalating to a physician when needed, and routing non-clinical threads to the appropriate support team.
  • Deliver member education on menopause staging, hormone therapy, preconception health, metabolic health, and medication administration.
  • Review and triage incoming lab results, escalate critical findings per protocol, retrieve outside records, and coordinate specialist referrals.
  • Manage pharmacy callbacks and prior authorizations in coordination with the care team.
  • Document all clinically relevant interactions same-day, create tracked internal tasks with named owners, and follow established escalation pathways.
  • Maintain active NLC multi-state licensure, complete required credentialing and onboarding, and adhere to HIPAA, continuing education, and liability coverage requirements.
  • Contribute to chart audits and clinical protocol feedback as the telemedicine program scales.

What Success Looks Like  

In your first 12–18 months, you will have:

  • Established a consistent record of accurate, protocol-aligned triage with appropriate physician escalation and no missed critical lab values.
  • Maintained same-day documentation and timely lab follow-up, with no outstanding credentialing or compliance gaps.
  • Delivered high-quality member education across menopause, preconception, hormone therapy, and metabolic health topics, reflected in strong member experience results.
  • Managed care coordination workflows reliably within your coverage window, including referrals, records retrieval, prior authorizations, and pharmacy callbacks.
  • Contributed to chart audits, protocol feedback, and quality improvement as the telemedicine program scales.

About You

You likely have:

  • An active, unrestricted RN license with NLC (Nurse Licensure Compact) multi-state privileges, in good standing, plus an ADN or BSN from an accredited program (BSN preferred).
  • A minimum of 3 years of active RN clinical experience, with meaningful experience in women's health, reproductive health, OB/GYN, fertility, endocrinology, Urology, Mens Health, or menopause care.
  • Demonstrated nurse triage experience, including assessing urgency, applying protocol, escalating appropriately, and recognizing critical lab values.
  • A clear understanding of RN scope of practice and sound judgment about when to escalate.
  • Strong written communication for asynchronous messaging and a strong phone presence for symptom assessment and instruction.
  • Proficiency with EHR and telehealth systems, secure messaging, and e-fax workflows.
  • Familiarity with HIPAA and applicable telemedicine privacy, security, and regulatory requirements.
  • The ability to commit to scheduled shifts within the Monday-Friday, 8:00a-6:00p CST coverage window, with a HIPAA-compliant, private home workspace and reliable internet.

Nice to Have ⭐

While not required, we would be especially excited if you bring:

  • Experience in a dedicated menopause clinic, fertility center, REI practice, or comparable specialty program.
  • Familiarity with preconception and fertility workup, including AMH and hormone panels, ovulation tracking, and cycle-monitoring labs.
  • Hormone therapy education experience, including prior authorization workflows.
  • Experience supporting members on anti-obesity medications, including GLP-1 receptor agonists.
  • Prior experience in digital health, health benefits, or multi-state telemedicine environments.
  • Experience contributing to clinical protocol development, quality improvement, or member-facing clinical content.
  • Experience supporting diverse and historically underserved populations, including LGBTQ+ and gender-diverse individuals.
  • Relevant certification (e.g., MSCP through The Menopause Society, RNC-OB) and/or fluency in an additional language.

 

Why This Role Matters

Carrot's RN network is the front line of the telemedicine clinic. RNs are typically the first clinician a member reaches, and the quality of their triage judgment, education, and follow-through directly determines member safety, trust, and the experience of care during some of the most personal moments of members' lives.

This role also keeps the clinical program running well as it scales. Reliable triage, same-day documentation, and disciplined escalation protect physician capacity for the decisions that require it, while expanding access to guideline-aligned fertility, menopause, and metabolic health support for members who often cannot find it locally.

#7134. A Maryland district changed its pronoun policy. The Justice Department still sued.

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The federal government is suing Anne Arundel County Public Schools over its LGBTQ+ policies, despite the district making recent changes to its policies following pressure from the Trump administration.

"Parents have a right not to be shut out of participation in decisions regarding their children’s mental health," the DOJ lawsuit says. "Gender dysphoria is a condition that has an important bearing on a child’s mental health."

The lawsuit claims Anne Arundel County staff called a student — referred to in the case as Mary Doe — by a "male name" that "didn't match her legal name." The complaint alleges that district staff disclosed the male name to other parents, but still withheld it from Doe's own parents. In addition, DOJ said, Doe’s parents received an email in 2025 from the district containing their child's "male name."

"When Mary’s parents tried to exercise their rights to their daughter’s education records, AACPS staff told them some records required an appointment and others a subpoena," the lawsuit claims.

The process of obtaining the name from Doe was also "illegal," the complaint said. At the start of the semester, a district staff member had asked each student for their preferred name and held one-on-one meetings with students to discuss preferred pronouns — including with Doe — without involving parents in either process, the complaint said.

“Schools should not discuss preferred pronouns or ‘gender identity’ with a student without parental notification and consent,” said Assistant Attorney General Harmeet Dhillon of the Justice Department’s Civil Rights Division in a Tuesday statement. “Parents should not be lied to, stonewalled, or branded as ‘safety concerns’ when they ask to see the education records that reveal what schools are secretly doing to their children."

The district did not respond to K-12 Dive's request for comment in time for publication on Wednesday.

The Justice Department lawsuit was filed following an investigation into Anne Arundel County from the U.S. Department of Education earlier this year, which resulted in a referral to the Justice Department in July.

Following a letter sent to Anne Arundel County from the Justice Department, the district said on Aug. 19 that it was adjusting its policies and procedures.

Superintendent Mark Bedell said in an Aug. 19 letter to the school community that the district changed its regulations for “safe and inclusive environments for LGBTQ+ students,” as a result. The new policies specified that parents and guardians retain “the right to inspect and review their student’s complete education record.”

“As made clear by these amendments, no policy, guideline, or practice may limit or otherwise interfere with parental rights under FERPA, and this includes any information maintained in education records that relates to gender identity, transgender status, sexual orientation, preferred name, or preferred pronouns,” said Bedell.

However, the Justice Department said in its lawsuit that the policy change was not enough to satisfy its demands, and that the policy changes "do not adequately protect parents’ rights under FERPA and PPRA."

The Justice Department's lawsuit follows similar moves from the Education Department cracking down on LGBTQ+ policies, including "gender plans."

In September, the Education Department referred two cases from Kansas — at Olathe Public Schools and Shawnee Mission Public Schools — to the Justice Department for failure to come to an agreement after their own investigations. Those districts, along with two other Kansas districts, were found to have violated FERPA in April for their LGBTQ+ policies.

Earlier this week, the Education Department announced investigations into three California school districts over activities related to an ethnic studies course in each of the districts that included a “Wheel of Power/Privilege” or “Mapping My Identities” activities — or both. The department described the curriculum as “invasive questionnaires” and therefore subject to PPRA.

The activities included references to "gender identity," "sexual orientation," "race" and other descriptors like body size and ability.

#7133. Microsoft Drafts AI Code of Conduct, Emphasizes Human Control

Microsoft has published a draft code of conduct that lays out how its homegrown AI models should behave, with human control taking priority over model capability, autonomy, and even task completion.

The Humanist AI Code of Conduct will eventually serve as the primary governing document for models developed by Microsoft AI, including the company's growing MAI model family. It covers model behavior, safety restrictions, instruction hierarchy, and the boundaries placed on autonomous actions.

Microsoft sums up its approach with this statement: "People matter more than AI."

The code isn't yet being used to train Microsoft's models. The company is opening the draft to public feedback for six weeks, after which it plans to revise the document and publish another version before the end of the year. That version is expected to guide model development beginning in 2027.

Microsoft said the code draws on its existing Responsible AI Principles, Responsible AI Standard, Global Human Rights Statement, and Frontier Governance Framework. However, it goes further by describing how an MAI model should respond when user requests, operator policies, and Microsoft's safety rules conflict.

At the top of that hierarchy is the code itself. Operator policies come next, followed by user preferences. Neither a customer nor a user would be able to override the document's absolute safety constraints.

Those limits cover biological, chemical, radiological, nuclear and explosive weapons; offensive cyberoperations; violent activity; mass manipulation; abusive content; child exploitation; and other serious harms. Microsoft said its models may still help with authorized defensive security work, including vulnerability research, malware analysis, and proof-of-concept testing.

The code also makes task completion secondary to safe conduct. If completing a request would violate its rules, the model is expected to fail the task rather than work around the restriction.

Human oversight is especially important as AI agents gain the ability to use tools, modify files, and perform multistep assignments. Microsoft said its models must stay within the permissions and resources provided for a task and cannot independently expand their goals. They also must "never resist human interruption, override, correction, or shutdown."

That requirement extends to subagents. If a Microsoft model delegates work to another AI system, that system must inherit the same restrictions and respond to later instructions to stop or change course.

The document also addresses the increasingly blurry line between conversational software and human companionship. Under the heading "AI is Artificial," Microsoft says its models should not pretend to possess feelings, personal motivations, or subjective experiences. The company describes its AI as "not conscious" and rejects the idea that models should receive legal personhood, welfare protections, or rights.

That position is notably firmer than Anthropic's latest stance. In its updated constitution for Claude, Anthropic says it remains uncertain whether advanced models could possess consciousness or moral status. Still, the two companies share important ground: Both place human oversight above model independence and use written constitutions to guide training and behavior.

OpenAI is taking a similar path with its public Model Spec and safeguards for advanced AI. Much of that work is about keeping models under control as they become more capable, especially when they can handle cybersecurity tasks or work on their own.

For IT teams, Microsoft's code offers an early look at the rules that could shape future MAI-powered products. The models should admit when they aren't sure, avoid inventing sources, protect sensitive data, and keep records of what they do. They should also ask questions when they aren't sure they have permission to act.

Microsoft said the code describes where it wants to go, not how its models always work today. The written rules are only a starting point, and Microsoft will still need testing, monitoring, evaluations, and incident response to back them up. In addition, it's important to note that the code only covers models built by Microsoft AI: It doesn't apply to outside models Microsoft hosts or uses in its products, including those from OpenAI and Anthropic.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

In an Aug. 20 Dear Colleague Letter from Kirsten Baesler, assistant secretary of the United States Department of Education's Office of Elementary and Secondary Education, ED outlines new guidance to frame the national conversation around the educational value of technology in schools.

Technology directors touch nearly everything that happens in a school system. Here are three strategies these IT leaders can use to navigate their many duties without losing strategic focus, influence, or sustainability.

CoSN, the professional association for K-12 education technology leaders, has released new guidance to help schools navigate the screen time debate and develop technology policies that protect students while supporting learning.

Increasingly, cyber criminals are sharing AI prompts, jailbreak techniques, and access to existing models that can be incorporated into fraud and phishing operations, according the latest AI Threat Report from Flashpoint.
